**Ronald Wilson Reagan**[a] (February 6, 1911 – June 5, 2004) was an American politician and actor who served as the 40th [president of the United States](/wiki/President_of_the_United_States "President of the United States") from 1981 to 1989. A member of the [Republican Party](/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party \(United States\)"), he became an important figure in the [American conservative movement](/wiki/American_conservative_movement "American conservative movement"). The period encompassing [his presidency](/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan "Presidency of Ronald Reagan") is known as the [Reagan era](/wiki/Reagan_era "Reagan era"). 

Born in [Tampico, Illinois](/wiki/Tampico,_Illinois "Tampico, Illinois"), Reagan graduated from [Eureka College](/wiki/Eureka_College "Eureka College") in 1932 and was hired the next year as a sports broadcaster in Iowa. In 1937, he moved to California where he became a well-known film actor. During his acting career, Reagan was president of the [Screen Actors Guild](/wiki/Screen_Actors_Guild "Screen Actors Guild") twice from 1947 to 1952 and from 1959 to 1960. In the 1950s, he hosted _[General Electric Theater](/wiki/General_Electric_Theater "General Electric Theater")_ and worked as a motivational speaker for [General Electric](/wiki/General_Electric "General Electric"). During the [1964 presidential election](/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election "1964 United States presidential election"), Reagan's "[A Time for Choosing](/wiki/A_Time_for_Choosing "A Time for Choosing")" speech launched his rise as a leading conservative figure. After being [elected governor of California in 1966](/wiki/1966_California_gubernatorial_election "1966 California gubernatorial election"), he raised state taxes, turned the state budget deficit into a surplus and implemented crackdowns on university protests. Following his loss to [Gerald Ford](/wiki/Gerald_Ford "Gerald Ford") in the [1976 Republican Party presidential primaries](/wiki/1976_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries "1976 Republican Party presidential primaries"), Reagan won the Republican Party's nomination and then obtained a landslide victory over President [Jimmy Carter](/wiki/Jimmy_Carter "Jimmy Carter") in the [1980 presidential election](/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election "1980 United States presidential election"). 

In his first term as president, Reagan began implementing "[Reaganomics](/wiki/Reaganomics "Reaganomics")", a policy involving economic deregulation and cuts to both taxes and government spending during [a period of stagflation](/wiki/Early_1980s_recession_in_the_United_States "Early 1980s recession in the United States"). On the world stage, he [escalated the arms race](/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative "Strategic Defense Initiative"), increased military spending, [shifted Cold War policy](/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine "Reagan Doctrine") away from _[détente](/wiki/D%C3%A9tente "Détente")_ , and ordered the [1983 invasion of Grenada](/wiki/1983_invasion_of_Grenada "1983 invasion of Grenada"). Reagan's first term included [his survival of an assassination attempt](/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Ronald_Reagan "Attempted assassination of Ronald Reagan"), a well-publicized fight with public-sector labor unions, an expansion of the [war on drugs](/wiki/War_on_drugs "War on drugs"), and [his slow response to the AIDS epidemic](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_and_AIDS "Ronald Reagan and AIDS"). In the [1984 presidential election](/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election "1984 United States presidential election"), he was elected to a second term by defeating former vice president [Walter Mondale](/wiki/Walter_Mondale "Walter Mondale") in [one of the largest landslide victories in American history](/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_elections_by_popular_vote_margin "List of United States presidential elections by popular vote margin"). Foreign affairs dominated his second term, including the [1986 bombing of Libya](/wiki/1986_bombing_of_Libya "1986 bombing of Libya"), the [secret and illegal sale of arms to Iran to fund the Contras](/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair "Iran–Contra affair"), and engaging in negotiations with Soviet leader [Mikhail Gorbachev](/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev "Mikhail Gorbachev"), which culminated in the [Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty](/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty "Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty"). 

Reagan left the presidency in 1989 with the American economy having experienced a significant reduction of inflation, a decline in the unemployment rate, and the longest peacetime economic expansion in US history at that time. The national debt had nearly tripled since 1981 as a result of his tax cuts and increased military spending outweighing his cuts to domestic discretionary spending. [Reagan's foreign policies](/wiki/Reagan%27s_foreign_policies "Reagan's foreign policies") also contributed to the end of the Cold War. Although he planned an active post-presidency, those plans were hindered after he was diagnosed with [Alzheimer's disease](/wiki/Alzheimer%27s_disease "Alzheimer's disease") in 1994. His physical and mental capacities gradually deteriorated leading to [his death](/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Ronald_Reagan "Death and state funeral of Ronald Reagan") in 2004. His tenure constituted a [realignment](/wiki/Realigning_election "Realigning election") toward conservative policies in the United States, and he is often considered an icon of American conservatism. [Historical rankings of US presidents](/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_US_presidents "Historical rankings of US presidents") have typically placed Reagan in the middle to upper tier, and [his post-presidential approval ratings by the general public](/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_the_Ronald_Reagan_administration "Opinion polling on the Ronald Reagan administration") have generally remained high.

  * [v](/wiki/Template:Ronald_Reagan_series "Template:Ronald Reagan series")
  * [t](/wiki/Template_talk:Ronald_Reagan_series "Template talk:Ronald Reagan series")
  * [e](/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Ronald_Reagan_series "Special:EditPage/Template:Ronald Reagan series")

  
  
Ronald Wilson Reagan was born on February 6, 1911, in [an apartment](/wiki/Birthplace_of_Ronald_Reagan "Birthplace of Ronald Reagan") in [Tampico, Illinois](/wiki/Tampico,_Illinois "Tampico, Illinois"), as the younger son of [Nelle Clyde Wilson](/wiki/Nelle_Wilson_Reagan "Nelle Wilson Reagan") and [Jack Reagan](/wiki/Jack_Reagan "Jack Reagan").[8] Nelle was committed to the [Disciples of Christ](/wiki/Disciples_of_Christ "Disciples of Christ"),[9] which believed in the [Social Gospel](/wiki/Social_Gospel "Social Gospel").[10] She led [prayer meetings](/wiki/Prayer_meeting "Prayer meeting") and ran mid-week prayers at her church when the pastor was out of town.[9] Reagan credited her spiritual influence[11] and he became a [Christian](/wiki/Christianity_in_the_United_States "Christianity in the United States").[12] According to American political figure [Stephen Vaughn](/wiki/Stephen_Vaughn "Stephen Vaughn"), Reagan's values came from his pastor, and the First Christian Church's religious, economic and social positions "coincided with the words, if not the beliefs of the latter-day Reagan".[13] Jack focused on making money to take care of the family,[8] but this was complicated by his alcoholism.[14] Reagan had an older brother, [Neil](/wiki/Neil_Reagan "Neil Reagan").[15] The family lived in Chicago, [Galesburg](/wiki/Galesburg,_Illinois "Galesburg, Illinois"), and [Monmouth](/wiki/Monmouth,_Illinois "Monmouth, Illinois") before returning to Tampico. In 1920, they settled in [Dixon, Illinois](/wiki/Dixon,_Illinois "Dixon, Illinois"),[16] living in [a house](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Boyhood_Home "Ronald Reagan Boyhood Home") near the [H. C. Pitney Variety Store Building](/wiki/H._C._Pitney_Variety_Store_Building "H. C. Pitney Variety Store Building").[17] Reagan attended [Dixon High School](/wiki/Dixon_High_School_\(Illinois\) "Dixon High School \(Illinois\)"), where he developed interests in drama and [football](/wiki/American_football "American football").[18] His first job was as a [lifeguard](/wiki/Lifeguard "Lifeguard") at the [Rock River](/wiki/Rock_River_\(Mississippi_River_tributary\) "Rock River \(Mississippi River tributary\)") in [Lowell Park](/wiki/Lowell_Park_\(Dixon,_Illinois\) "Lowell Park \(Dixon, Illinois\)").[19]

In 1928, Reagan began attending [Eureka College](/wiki/Eureka_College "Eureka College"),[20] which Nelle approved because of its affiliation with the Disciples of Christ.[21] He was a mediocre student[22] who participated in [cheerleading](/wiki/Cheerleading "Cheerleading"),[23] sports, drama, and campus politics. He became [student body president](/wiki/Student_body_president "Student body president") and joined a student strike that resulted in the college president's resignation.[24] Reagan was initiated as a member of [Tau Kappa Epsilon](/wiki/Tau_Kappa_Epsilon "Tau Kappa Epsilon") fraternity and served as president of the local chapter.[25] Reagan played at the [guard](/wiki/Guard_\(American_football\) "Guard \(American football\)") position for the [1930](/wiki/1930_Eureka_Red_Devils_football_team "1930 Eureka Red Devils football team") and [1931 Eureka Red Devils football teams](/wiki/1931_Eureka_Red_Devils_football_team "1931 Eureka Red Devils football team") and recalled a time when two Black teammates were refused service at a [segregated](/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States "Racial segregation in the United States") hotel; he invited them to his parents' home nearby in Dixon and his parents welcomed them. At the time, his parents' stance on racial questions was unusually [progressive](/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States "Progressivism in the United States") in Dixon.[26] Reagan himself had grown up with very few [Black Americans](/wiki/Black_Americans "Black Americans") and felt oblivious to racial discrimination.[27]

## Entertainment career

Further information: [Ronald Reagan filmography](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_filmography "Ronald Reagan filmography")

### Radio and film

[](/wiki/File:Ronald_Reagan_in_Dark_Victory_trailer.jpg)

_[Dark Victory](/wiki/Dark_Victory "Dark Victory")_ (1939)

[](/wiki/File:Ronald_Reagan_in_The_Bad_Man_\(1941\).png)

_[The Bad Man](/wiki/The_Bad_Man_\(1941_film\) "The Bad Man \(1941 film\)")_ (1941)

After obtaining a [Bachelor of Arts](/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts "Bachelor of Arts") in economics and sociology from [Eureka College](/wiki/Eureka_College "Eureka College") in 1932,[28][29] Reagan took a job in [Davenport, Iowa](/wiki/Davenport,_Iowa "Davenport, Iowa"), as a sports broadcaster for four football games in the [Big Ten Conference](/wiki/Big_Ten_Conference "Big Ten Conference").[30] He then worked for [WHO radio](/wiki/WHO_\(AM\) "WHO \(AM\)") in [Des Moines](/wiki/Des_Moines "Des Moines") as a broadcaster for the [Chicago Cubs](/wiki/Chicago_Cubs "Chicago Cubs"). His specialty was creating play-by-play accounts of games using only basic descriptions that the station received by wire as the games were in progress.[31] Simultaneously, he often expressed his opposition to racism.[32] In 1936, while traveling with the Cubs to their spring training in California, Reagan took a screen test that led to a seven-year contract with [Warner Bros. Pictures](/wiki/Warner_Bros._Pictures "Warner Bros. Pictures").[33]

Reagan arrived at [Hollywood](/wiki/Hollywood,_Los_Angeles "Hollywood, Los Angeles") in 1937, debuting in _[Love Is on the Air](/wiki/Love_Is_on_the_Air "Love Is on the Air")_ (1937).[34] Using a simple and direct approach to acting and following his directors' instructions,[35] he made thirty films, mostly [B films](/wiki/B_films "B films"), before beginning [military service](/wiki/Military_history_of_the_United_States_during_World_War_II "Military history of the United States during World War II") in April 1942.[36] He broke out of these types of films by portraying [George Gipp](/wiki/George_Gipp "George Gipp") in _[Knute Rockne, All American](/wiki/Knute_Rockne,_All_American "Knute Rockne, All American")_ (1940), which would be rejuvenated when reporters called Reagan "the Gipper" while he campaigned for president.[37] Reagan starred in _[Kings Row](/wiki/Kings_Row "Kings Row")_ (1942) as a leg amputee;[38] this performance was considered his best by many critics.[39] Reagan became a star,[40] with [Gallup polls](/wiki/Gallup_polls "Gallup polls") placing him "in the top 100 stars" from 1941 to 1942.[39]

[World War II](/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") interrupted the movie stardom that Reagan would never be able to achieve again[40] as Warner Bros. became uncertain about his ability to generate ticket sales. Reagan, who had a limited acting range, was dissatisfied with the roles he received. [Lew Wasserman](/wiki/Lew_Wasserman "Lew Wasserman") renegotiated his contract with his studio, allowing him to also make films with [Universal Pictures](/wiki/Universal_Pictures "Universal Pictures"), [Paramount Pictures](/wiki/Paramount_Pictures "Paramount Pictures"), and [RKO Pictures](/wiki/RKO_Pictures "RKO Pictures") as a freelancer. Reagan appeared in multiple [western films](/wiki/Western_film "Western film"), something that had been denied to him while working at Warner Bros.[41] In 1952, he ended his relationship with Warner Bros.,[42] but went on to appear in a total of 53 films,[36] his last being _[The Killers](/wiki/The_Killers_\(1964_film\) "The Killers \(1964 film\)")_ (1964).[43]

### Military service

[](/wiki/File:Reagan_FMPU.jpg)Reagan at [Fort Roach](/wiki/First_Motion_Picture_Unit#Life_at_Fort_Roach "First Motion Picture Unit"), between 1943 and 1944

In April 1937, Reagan enlisted in the [United States Army Reserve](/wiki/United_States_Army_Reserve "United States Army Reserve"). He was assigned as a [private](/wiki/Private_\(rank\) "Private \(rank\)") in Des Moines' [322nd Cavalry Regiment](/wiki/322nd_Cavalry_Regiment_\(United_States\) "322nd Cavalry Regiment \(United States\)") and reassigned to [second lieutenant](/wiki/Second_lieutenant "Second lieutenant") in the Officers Reserve Corps.[44] He later became a part of the [323rd Cavalry Regiment](/wiki/323rd_Cavalry_Regiment_\(United_States\) "323rd Cavalry Regiment \(United States\)") in California.[45] As relations between the United States and [Japan](/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") worsened, Reagan was ordered for active duty while he was filming _[Kings Row](/wiki/Kings_Row "Kings Row")_. Wasserman and Warner Bros. lawyers successfully sent draft deferments to complete the film in October 1941. However, to avoid accusations of Reagan being a [draft dodger](/wiki/Draft_dodger "Draft dodger"), the studio let him go in April 1942.[46]

Reagan reported for duty with severe [near-sightedness](/wiki/Near-sightedness "Near-sightedness"). His first assignment was at [Fort Mason](/wiki/Fort_Mason "Fort Mason") as a [liaison officer](/wiki/Liaison_officer "Liaison officer"), a role that allowed him to transfer to the [United States Army Air Forces](/wiki/United_States_Army_Air_Forces "United States Army Air Forces") (AAF). Reagan became an AAF [public relations officer](/wiki/Public_relations_officer "Public relations officer") and was assigned to the [18th AAF Base Unit](/wiki/18th_AAF_Base_Unit "18th AAF Base Unit") in [Culver City](/wiki/Culver_City "Culver City")[47] where he felt that it was "impossible to remove an incompetent or lazy worker" due to what he felt was "the incompetence, the delays, and inefficiencies" of the federal [bureaucracy](/wiki/Bureaucracy "Bureaucracy").[48] Despite this, Reagan participated in the Provisional Task Force Show Unit in [Burbank](/wiki/Burbank,_California "Burbank, California")[49] and continued to make theatrical films.[50] He was also ordered to temporary duty in New York City to participate in the [sixth War Loan Drive](/wiki/Series_E_bond "Series E bond") before being reassigned to [Fort MacArthur](/wiki/Fort_MacArthur "Fort MacArthur") until his discharge on December 9, 1945, as a [captain](/wiki/Captain_\(United_States_O-3\) "Captain \(United States O-3\)"). Throughout his military service, Reagan produced over 400 [training films](/wiki/Training_film "Training film").[49]

### Screen Actors Guild presidency

[](/wiki/File:Telephone_conference_during_1946_CSU_strike.jpg)Seven actors and studio workers during a telephone conference held in the aftermath of [Hollywood Black Friday](/wiki/Hollywood_Black_Friday "Hollywood Black Friday") in which [American Federation of Labor](/wiki/American_Federation_of_Labor "American Federation of Labor") officials denied issuing a "clarification" which set off the film strike, October 26, 1946.  
**(L-R):** James Skelton, [Herbert Sorrell](/wiki/Herbert_Sorrell "Herbert Sorrell"), **Ronald Reagan** , [Edward Arnold](/wiki/Edward_Arnold_\(actor\) "Edward Arnold \(actor\)"), Roy Tindall, [George Murphy](/wiki/George_Murphy "George Murphy"), and [Gene Kelly](/wiki/Gene_Kelly "Gene Kelly").

When [Robert Montgomery](/wiki/Robert_Montgomery_\(actor\) "Robert Montgomery \(actor\)") resigned as president of the [Screen Actors Guild](/wiki/Screen_Actors_Guild "Screen Actors Guild") (SAG) on March 10, 1947, Reagan was elected to that position in a special election.[51] Reagan's first tenure saw various labor–management disputes,[52] the [Hollywood blacklist](/wiki/Hollywood_blacklist "Hollywood blacklist"),[53] and the [Taft–Hartley Act](/wiki/Taft%E2%80%93Hartley_Act "Taft–Hartley Act")'s implementation.[54] Reagan aligned the union with the studios against the Conference of Studio Unions in the aftermath of the [Hollywood Black Friday](/wiki/Hollywood_Black_Friday "Hollywood Black Friday") strike. In _The Invisible Bridge_ , [Rick Perlstein](/wiki/Rick_Perlstein "Rick Perlstein") wrote that Reagan's actions lent legitimacy to the studio's efforts to crush the more radical union by giving liberals in SAG who did not want to strike "a story that turned them into moral innocents instead of scabs".[55] On April 10, the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](/wiki/Federal_Bureau_of_Investigation "Federal Bureau of Investigation") (FBI) interviewed Reagan and he provided the names of actors whom he believed to be [communist sympathizers](/wiki/Communist_sympathizers "Communist sympathizers").[56] During a [House Un-American Activities Committee](/wiki/House_Un-American_Activities_Committee "House Un-American Activities Committee") hearing, Reagan testified that some guild members were associated with the [Communist Party](/wiki/Communist_Party_USA "Communist Party USA")[57] and that he was well-informed about a "jurisdictional strike".[58] When asked if he was aware of communist efforts within the [Screen Writers Guild](/wiki/Screen_Writers_Guild "Screen Writers Guild"), he called information about the efforts "hearsay".[59] Reagan resigned as SAG president November 10, 1952, but remained on the board.[60]

The SAG fought with film producers for the right to receive [residual payments](/wiki/Residual_\(entertainment_industry\) "Residual \(entertainment industry\)"),[61] and on November 16, 1959, the board elected Reagan SAG president for the second time.[62] Reagan managed to secure payments for actors whose theatrical films had been released between 1948 and 1959 and subsequently televised. The producers were initially required to pay the actors fees, but they ultimately settled instead for providing pensions and paying residuals for films made after 1959. Reagan resigned from the SAG presidency on June 7, 1960, and also left the board.[63]

### Marriages and children

[](/wiki/File:Wyman_%26_Reagan.jpg)

Reagan and Jane Wyman, 1942

[](/wiki/File:The_Reagan%27s_at_the_Stork_Club_in_New_York_City.jpg)

Ronald and Nancy Reagan, 1952

In January 1940, Reagan married [Jane Wyman](/wiki/Jane_Wyman "Jane Wyman"), his co-star in the 1938 film _[Brother Rat](/wiki/Brother_Rat "Brother Rat")_.[64][65] Together, they had two biological daughters: [Maureen](/wiki/Maureen_Reagan "Maureen Reagan") in 1941,[66] and Christine in 1947 (born prematurely and died the following day).[67] They adopted one son, [Michael](/wiki/Michael_Reagan "Michael Reagan"), in 1945.[48] Wyman filed for divorce in June 1948. She was uninterested in politics, and occasionally recriminated, reconciled and [separated](/wiki/Marital_separation "Marital separation") with him. Although Reagan was unprepared,[67] the divorce was finalized in July 1949.[68] Later that year, Reagan met [Nancy Davis](/wiki/Nancy_Davis "Nancy Davis") after she contacted him in his capacity as the SAG president about her name appearing on a communist [blacklist in Hollywood](/wiki/Blacklist_in_Hollywood "Blacklist in Hollywood"); she had been mistaken for another Nancy Davis.[69] They married in March 1952,[70] and had two children, [Patti](/wiki/Patti_Davis "Patti Davis") in October 1952, and [Ron](/wiki/Ron_Reagan "Ron Reagan") in May 1958.[71]

### Television

Reagan became the host of [MCA Inc.](/wiki/MCA_Inc. "MCA Inc.") television production _[General Electric Theater](/wiki/General_Electric_Theater "General Electric Theater")_[42] at Wasserman's recommendation. It featured multiple guest stars,[72] and Ronald and Nancy Reagan, continuing to use her stage name Nancy Davis, acted together in three episodes.[73] When asked how Reagan was able to recruit such stars to appear on the show during television's infancy, he replied, "Good stories, top direction, production quality".[74] However, the viewership declined in the 1960s and the show was canceled in 1962.[75] In 1965, Reagan became the host[76] of another MCA production, _[Death Valley Days](/wiki/Death_Valley_Days "Death Valley Days")_.[77]

## Early political activities

[](/wiki/File:Goldwater-Reagan_in_1964.jpg)Reagan [campaigning](/wiki/Barry_Goldwater_1964_presidential_campaign "Barry Goldwater 1964 presidential campaign") with [Barry Goldwater](/wiki/Barry_Goldwater "Barry Goldwater"), 1964

Reagan began his political career as a [Democrat](/wiki/Democratic_Party_\(United_States\) "Democratic Party \(United States\)"), viewing [Franklin D. Roosevelt](/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt "Franklin D. Roosevelt") as "a true hero".[78] He joined the [American Veterans Committee](/wiki/American_Veterans_Committee_\(1943%E2%80%932008\) "American Veterans Committee \(1943–2008\)") and [Hollywood Independent Citizens Committee of the Arts, Sciences and Professions](/wiki/Hollywood_Independent_Citizens_Committee_of_the_Arts,_Sciences_and_Professions "Hollywood Independent Citizens Committee of the Arts, Sciences and Professions") (HICCASP), worked with the [AFL–CIO](/wiki/AFL%E2%80%93CIO "AFL–CIO") to fight [right-to-work laws](/wiki/Right-to-work_law "Right-to-work law"),[79] and continued to speak out against racism when he was in Hollywood.[80] In 1945, Reagan planned to lead an HICCASP anti-nuclear rally, but Warner Bros. prevented him from going.[81] In 1946, he appeared in a radio program called _Operation Terror_ to speak out against rising [Ku Klux Klan](/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan") activity, calling it a "capably organized systematic campaign of fascist violence and intimidation and horror".[82] Reagan supported [Harry S. Truman](/wiki/Harry_S._Truman "Harry S. Truman") in the [1948 presidential election](/wiki/1948_United_States_presidential_election "1948 United States presidential election"),[83] and [Helen Gahagan Douglas](/wiki/Helen_Gahagan_Douglas "Helen Gahagan Douglas") for the [US Senate in 1950](/wiki/1950_United_States_Senate_election_in_California "1950 United States Senate election in California"). It was Reagan's belief that communism was a powerful backstage influence in Hollywood that led him to rally his friends against them.[79]

Reagan began shifting to the right when he supported the presidential campaigns of [Dwight D. Eisenhower](/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower "Dwight D. Eisenhower") in 1952 and [Richard Nixon](/wiki/Richard_Nixon "Richard Nixon") in 1960.[84] When Reagan was contracted by [General Electric](/wiki/General_Electric "General Electric") (GE), he gave speeches to their employees. His speeches had a positive take on [free markets](/wiki/Free_market "Free market").[85] In 1961, Reagan adapted his speeches into [another speech](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Speaks_Out_Against_Socialized_Medicine "Ronald Reagan Speaks Out Against Socialized Medicine") to criticize [Medicare](/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\) "Medicare \(United States\)").[86] In his view, its legislation would have meant "the end of [individual freedom](/wiki/Individual_freedom "Individual freedom") in the United States".[87] In 1962, Reagan was dropped by GE,[88] and he formally registered as a [Republican](/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party \(United States\)").[84]

In the [1964 US presidential election](/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election "1964 United States presidential election"), Reagan gave a speech for presidential contender [Barry Goldwater](/wiki/Barry_Goldwater "Barry Goldwater")[89] that was eventually referred to as "[A Time for Choosing](/wiki/A_Time_for_Choosing "A Time for Choosing")".[90] Reagan argued that the [Founding Fathers](/wiki/Founding_Fathers "Founding Fathers") "knew that governments don't control things. And they knew when a government sets out to do that, it must use force and coercion to achieve its purpose"[91] and that "We've been told increasingly that we must choose between left or right".[92] Even though the speech was not enough to turn around the faltering [Goldwater campaign](/wiki/Barry_Goldwater_1964_presidential_campaign "Barry Goldwater 1964 presidential campaign"), it increased Reagan's profile among conservatives. [David S. Broder](/wiki/David_S._Broder "David S. Broder") and [Stephen H. Hess](/wiki/Stephen_H._Hess "Stephen H. Hess") called it "the most successful national political debut since [William Jennings Bryan](/wiki/William_Jennings_Bryan "William Jennings Bryan") electrified the [1896 Democratic convention](/wiki/1896_Democratic_National_Convention "1896 Democratic National Convention") with his famous ['Cross of Gold' address](/wiki/Cross_of_Gold_speech "Cross of Gold speech")".[89]

### 1966 California gubernatorial election

Further information: [1966 California gubernatorial election](/wiki/1966_California_gubernatorial_election "1966 California gubernatorial election")

[](/wiki/File:Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_at_victory_celebration_for_1966_Governor%27s_election_\(cropped\).jpg)Ronald and Nancy Reagan celebrating his gubernatorial election victory, November 1966

In January 1966, Reagan announced his candidacy for the [California governorship](/wiki/Governor_of_California "Governor of California"),[93] repeating his stances on individual freedom and [big government](/wiki/Big_government "Big government").[94] When he met with black Republicans in March,[95] he was criticized for opposing the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964 "Civil Rights Act of 1964"). Reagan responded that bigotry was not in his nature[96] and later argued that certain provisions of the act infringed upon the rights of property owners.[97] After the [Supreme Court of California](/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_California "Supreme Court of California") ruled that the initiative that repealed the [Rumford Act](/wiki/Rumford_Act "Rumford Act") was unconstitutional in May, he voiced his support for the act's repeal,[98] but later preferred amending it.[99] In the Republican primary, Reagan defeated [George Christopher](/wiki/George_Christopher_\(mayor\) "George Christopher \(mayor\)"),[100] a moderate Republican[101] who [William F. Buckley Jr.](/wiki/William_F._Buckley_Jr. "William F. Buckley Jr.") thought had painted Reagan as extreme.[94]

Reagan's general election opponent, incumbent governor [Pat Brown](/wiki/Pat_Brown "Pat Brown"), attempted to label Reagan as an extremist.[102] Reagan portrayed himself as a political outsider,[103] and charged Brown as responsible for the [Watts riots](/wiki/Watts_riots "Watts riots") and lenient on crime.[102] In numerous speeches, Reagan "hit the Brown administration about high taxes, uncontrolled spending, the radicals at the [University of California, Berkeley](/wiki/University_of_California,_Berkeley "University of California, Berkeley"), and the need for [accountability](/wiki/Accountability "Accountability") in government".[104] Meanwhile, many in the press perceived Reagan as "monumentally ignorant of state issues", though [Lou Cannon](/wiki/Lou_Cannon "Lou Cannon") said that Reagan benefited from an appearance he and Brown made on _[Meet the Press](/wiki/Meet_the_Press "Meet the Press")_ in September.[105] Reagan won the governorship with 58% of the vote compared to Brown's 42%.[106]

## California governorship (1967–1975)

Main article: [Governorship of Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Governorship_of_Ronald_Reagan "Governorship of Ronald Reagan")

[](/wiki/File:Ronald_Reagan_and_Nancy_Reagan_at_airport,_1972_restored.jpg)The Reagans in 1972

Brown had spent much of California's funds on new programs, prompting them to use [accrual accounting](/wiki/Accrual_accounting "Accrual accounting") to avoid raising taxes. Consequently, it generated a larger deficit,[107] and Reagan called for reduced government spending and tax hikes to [balance the budget](/wiki/Balance_the_budget "Balance the budget").[108] He worked with [Jesse M. Unruh](/wiki/Jesse_M._Unruh "Jesse M. Unruh") on securing tax increases and promising future property tax cuts. This caused some conservatives to accuse Reagan of betraying his principles.[109] As a result, taxes on sales, banks, corporate profits, inheritances, liquor, and cigarettes jumped. [Kevin Starr](/wiki/Kevin_Starr "Kevin Starr") states Reagan "gave Californians the biggest tax hike in their history—and got away with it".[110] In [the 1970 gubernatorial election](/wiki/1970_California_gubernatorial_election "1970 California gubernatorial election"), Unruh used Reagan's tax policy against him, saying it disproportionally favored the wealthy. Reagan countered that he was still committed to reducing property taxes.[111] By 1973, the budget had a surplus, which Reagan preferred "to give back to the people".[112]

In 1967, Reagan reacted to the [Black Panther Party](/wiki/Black_Panther_Party "Black Panther Party")'s strategy of [copwatching](/wiki/Copwatch "Copwatch") by signing the [Mulford Act](/wiki/Mulford_Act "Mulford Act")[113] to prohibit the public carrying of firearms. The act was California's most restrictive piece of [gun control legislation](/wiki/Gun_politics_in_the_United_States "Gun politics in the United States"), with critics saying that it was "overreacting to the political activism of organizations such as the Black Panthers".[114] The act marked the beginning of both modern legislation and [public attitude studies](/wiki/Public_opinion_on_gun_control_in_the_United_States "Public opinion on gun control in the United States") on gun control.[113] Reagan also signed the 1967 Therapeutic Abortion Act that allowed abortions in the cases of rape and incest when a doctor determined the birth would impair the physical or mental health of the mother. He later expressed regret over signing it, saying that he was unaware of the mental health provision. He believed that doctors were interpreting the provision loosely, resulting in more abortions.[115]

After Reagan won the 1966 election, he and his advisors planned a run in the [1968 Republican presidential primaries](/wiki/1968_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries "1968 Republican Party presidential primaries").[116] He ran as an unofficial candidate to cut into Nixon's southern support and be a compromise candidate if there were to be a [brokered convention](/wiki/Brokered_convention "Brokered convention"). He won California's delegates,[117] but Nixon secured enough delegates for [the nomination](/wiki/1968_Republican_National_Convention "1968 Republican National Convention").[118]

Reagan had previously been critical of former governor Brown and university administrators for tolerating student demonstrations in the city of [Berkeley](/wiki/Berkeley,_California "Berkeley, California"), making it a major theme in his campaigning.[119] On February 5, 1969, Reagan declared a state of emergency in response to [ongoing protests](/wiki/Free_Speech_Movement#1966–1970 "Free Speech Movement") and violence at the University of California, Berkeley, and sent in the [California Highway Patrol](/wiki/California_Highway_Patrol "California Highway Patrol"). In May 1969, these officers, along with local officers from Berkeley and Alameda county, [clashed with protestors](/wiki/1969_People%27s_Park_protest "1969 People's Park protest") over a site known as the [People's Park](/wiki/People%27s_Park_\(Berkeley\) "People's Park \(Berkeley\)").[120][121] One student was shot and killed while many police officers and two reporters were injured. Reagan then commanded the [state National Guard troops](/wiki/California_National_Guard "California National Guard") to occupy Berkeley for seventeen days to subdue the protesters, allowing other students to attend class safely. In February 1970, violent protests broke out near the [University of California, Santa Barbara](/wiki/University_of_California,_Santa_Barbara "University of California, Santa Barbara"), where he once again deployed the National Guard. On April 7, Reagan defended his policies regarding campus protests, saying, "If it takes a bloodbath, let's get it over with. No more appeasement".[122]

During his victorious reelection campaign in 1970, Reagan, remaining critical of government, promised to prioritize [welfare reform](/wiki/Welfare_reform "Welfare reform").[123] He was concerned that the programs were disincentivizing work and that the growing welfare rolls would lead to both an unbalanced budget and another big tax hike in 1972.[124] At the same time, the [Federal Reserve](/wiki/Federal_Reserve "Federal Reserve") increased interest rates to combat inflation, putting the American economy in [a mild recession](/wiki/Recession_of_1969%E2%80%931970 "Recession of 1969–1970"). Reagan worked with [Bob Moretti](/wiki/Bob_Moretti "Bob Moretti") to tighten up the eligibility requirements so that the financially needy could continue receiving payments. This was only accomplished after Reagan softened his criticism of Nixon's [Family Assistance Plan](/wiki/Family_Assistance_Plan "Family Assistance Plan"). Nixon then lifted regulations to shepherd California's experiment.[125] In 1976, the [Employment Development Department](/wiki/Employment_Development_Department "Employment Development Department") published a report suggesting that the experiment that ran from 1971 to 1974 was unsuccessful.[126]

Reagan declined to run for the governorship [in 1974](/wiki/1974_California_gubernatorial_election "1974 California gubernatorial election") and it was won by Pat Brown's son, [Jerry](/wiki/Jerry_Brown "Jerry Brown").[127] Reagan's governorship, as professor Gary K. Clabaugh writes, saw public schools deteriorate due to his opposition to additional basic education funding.[128] As for higher education, journalist William Trombley believed that the budget cuts Reagan enacted damaged Berkeley's student-faculty ratio and research.[129] The homicide rate doubled and armed robbery rates rose by even more during Reagan's eight years, even with the many laws Reagan signed to try toughening criminal sentencing and reforming the criminal justice system.[130] Reagan strongly supported capital punishment, but his efforts to enforce it were thwarted by _[People v. Anderson](/wiki/People_v._Anderson "People v. Anderson")_ in 1972.[131] According to his son, Michael, Reagan said that he regretted signing the Family Law Act that granted [no-fault divorces](/wiki/No-fault_divorce "No-fault divorce").[132]

Literature _[The Federalist Papers](/wiki/The_Federalist_Papers "The Federalist Papers")_ (1788) _[Democracy in America](/wiki/Democracy_in_America "Democracy in America")_ (1835–1840) _[Notes on Democracy](/wiki/Notes_on_Democracy "Notes on Democracy")_ (1926) _[I'll Take My Stand](/wiki/Southern_Agrarians#I'll_Take_My_Stand "Southern Agrarians")_ (1930) _[The Managerial Revolution](/wiki/The_Managerial_Revolution "The Managerial Revolution")_ (1941) _[Ideas Have Consequences](/wiki/Ideas_Have_Consequences "Ideas Have Consequences")_ (1948) _[God and Man at Yale](/wiki/God_and_Man_at_Yale "God and Man at Yale")_ (1951) _[The Conservative Mind](/wiki/The_Conservative_Mind "The Conservative Mind")_ (1953) _[The Conscience of a Conservative](/wiki/The_Conscience_of_a_Conservative "The Conscience of a Conservative")_ (1960) _[A Choice Not an Echo](/wiki/A_Choice_Not_an_Echo "A Choice Not an Echo")_ (1964) _[A Conflict of Visions](/wiki/A_Conflict_of_Visions "A Conflict of Visions")_ (1987) _[The Closing of the American Mind](/wiki/The_Closing_of_the_American_Mind "The Closing of the American Mind")_ (1987) _[The Death of the West](/wiki/The_Death_of_the_West "The Death of the West")_ (2001) _[Hillbilly Elegy](/wiki/Hillbilly_Elegy "Hillbilly Elegy")_ (2016) _[The Benedict Option](/wiki/The_Benedict_Option "The Benedict Option")_ (2017) _[Why Liberalism Failed](/wiki/Why_Liberalism_Failed "Why Liberalism Failed")_ (2018)

### 1976 Republican primaries

Main articles: [Ronald Reagan 1976 presidential campaign](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_1976_presidential_campaign "Ronald Reagan 1976 presidential campaign") and [1976 Republican Party presidential primaries](/wiki/1976_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries "1976 Republican Party presidential primaries")

[](/wiki/File:1976_Republican_National_Convention.jpg)Reagan and Gerald Ford shaking hands on the podium after Reagan narrowly lost the nomination at the [1976 Republican National Convention](/wiki/1976_Republican_National_Convention "1976 Republican National Convention")

Insufficiently conservative to Reagan[133] and many other Republicans,[134] President [Gerald Ford](/wiki/Gerald_Ford "Gerald Ford") suffered from multiple political and economic woes. Ford, running for president, was disappointed to hear him also run.[135] Reagan was strongly critical of _détente_ and Ford's policy of _détente_ with the Soviet Union.[136] He repeated "A Time for Choosing" around the country[137] before announcing his campaign on November 20, 1975, when he discussed economic and social problems, and to a lesser extent, foreign affairs.[138] Both candidates were determined to knock each other out early in the primaries,[139] but Reagan would devastatingly lose the first five primaries beginning with New Hampshire,[140] where he popularized the [welfare queen](/wiki/Welfare_queen "Welfare queen") narrative about [Linda Taylor](/wiki/Linda_Taylor "Linda Taylor"), exaggerating her misuse of welfare benefits and igniting voter resentment for welfare reform,[141] but never overtly mentioning her name or race.[142]

In Florida, Reagan used [racially coded rhetoric](/wiki/Dog_whistle_politics "Dog whistle politics") to undermine [Food Stamps](/wiki/Food_Stamps "Food Stamps"), referring to recipients as "strapping young buck[s]",[143][144] and attacked Ford for handing the [Panama Canal](/wiki/Panama_Canal "Panama Canal") to Panama's government, while Ford implied that Reagan would [end Social Security](/wiki/Social_Security_debate_in_the_United_States "Social Security debate in the United States").[140] Then, in Illinois, Reagan again criticized Ford's policy and his secretary of state, [Henry Kissinger](/wiki/Henry_Kissinger "Henry Kissinger").[145] Losing the first five primaries prompted Reagan to desperately win North Carolina's by running a grassroots campaign and uniting with the [Jesse Helms](/wiki/Jesse_Helms "Jesse Helms") political machine that viciously attacked Ford. Reagan won an upset victory, convincing party delegates that Ford's nomination was no longer guaranteed.[146] Reagan won subsequent victories in Texas, Alabama, Georgia, and Indiana with his attacks on social programs, opposition to [forced busing](/wiki/Forced_busing "Forced busing") to achieve school [desegregation](/wiki/Desegregation_in_the_United_States "Desegregation in the United States"),[147] and repeated criticisms of Ford and Kissinger's policies, including _détente_.[148]

Reagan's concession speech at the 1976 convention

The result was a seesaw battle for the 1,130 delegates required for their party's nomination that neither would reach before the [Kansas City convention](/wiki/1976_Republican_National_Convention "1976 Republican National Convention")[149] in August[150] and Ford replacing mentions of _détente_ with Reagan's preferred phrase, "[peace through strength](/wiki/Peace_through_strength "Peace through strength")".[151] Reagan took [John Sears](/wiki/John_Sears_\(political_strategist\) "John Sears \(political strategist\)")' advice of choosing liberal [Richard Schweiker](/wiki/Richard_Schweiker "Richard Schweiker") as his running mate, hoping to pry loose of delegates from Pennsylvania and other states, and distract Ford. Instead, conservatives were left alienated, and Ford picked up the remaining uncommitted delegates, earning 1,187 to Reagan's 1,070.[152]

After giving his acceptance speech, Ford invited Reagan to address the convention. Reagan gave an eloquent and stirring speech that overshadowed Ford's own acceptance address, despite being little more than five minutes long. Some delegates later stated that they left the convention wondering if they had voted for the wrong candidate.[153] A contemporary media account stated that if a motion to reconsider the nomination had been in order, it might have passed.[154]

In 1977, Ford told Cannon that Reagan's primary challenge contributed to his own narrow loss to Democrat [Jimmy Carter](/wiki/Jimmy_Carter "Jimmy Carter") in the [1976 presidential election](/wiki/1976_United_States_presidential_election "1976 United States presidential election").[155]

### 1980 election

Main article: [Ronald Reagan 1980 presidential campaign](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_1980_presidential_campaign "Ronald Reagan 1980 presidential campaign")

Further information: [1980 Republican Party presidential primaries](/wiki/1980_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries "1980 Republican Party presidential primaries") and [1980 United States presidential election](/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election "1980 United States presidential election")

[](/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1980.svg)1980 electoral vote results. Reagan won 489–49.

Reagan emerged as a vocal critic of President Carter in 1977. The [Panama Canal Treaty](/wiki/Panama_Canal_Treaty "Panama Canal Treaty")'s signing, the [1979 oil crisis](/wiki/1979_oil_crisis "1979 oil crisis"), and rise in the interest, [inflation and unemployment rates](/wiki/Misery_index_\(economics\) "Misery index \(economics\)") helped set up his 1980 presidential campaign,[156] which he announced on November 13, 1979[157] with an indictment of the federal government.[158] His announcement stressed his fundamental principles of tax cuts to stimulate the economy and having both a [small government](/wiki/Small_government "Small government") and a strong [national defense](/wiki/National_defense "National defense"),[159] since he believed the United States was behind the Soviet Union militarily.[160] Heading into 1980, his age became an issue among the press, and the United States was in [a severe recession](/wiki/Early_1980s_recession "Early 1980s recession").[161]

In the [primaries](/wiki/1980_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries "1980 Republican Party presidential primaries"), Reagan unexpectedly lost the [Iowa caucus](/wiki/Iowa_caucus "Iowa caucus") to [George H. W. Bush](/wiki/George_H._W._Bush "George H. W. Bush"). Three days before the [New Hampshire primary](/wiki/New_Hampshire_primary "New Hampshire primary"), the Reagan and Bush campaigns agreed to a one-on-one debate sponsored by _[The Telegraph](/wiki/The_Telegraph_\(Nashua,_New_Hampshire\) "The Telegraph \(Nashua, New Hampshire\)")_ at [Nashua, New Hampshire](/wiki/Nashua,_New_Hampshire "Nashua, New Hampshire"), but hours before the debate, the Reagan campaign invited other candidates including [Bob Dole](/wiki/Bob_Dole "Bob Dole"), [John B. Anderson](/wiki/John_B._Anderson "John B. Anderson"), [Howard Baker](/wiki/Howard_Baker "Howard Baker") and [Phil Crane](/wiki/Phil_Crane "Phil Crane").[162] Debate moderator Jon Breen denied seats to the other candidates, asserting that _The Telegraph_ would violate federal campaign contribution laws if it sponsored the debate and changed the ground rules hours before the debate.[163] As a result, the Reagan campaign agreed to pay for the debate. Reagan said that as he was funding the debate, he could decide who would debate.[164] During the debate, when Breen was laying out the ground rules and attempting to ask the first question, Reagan interrupted in protest to make an introductory statement and wanted other candidates to be included before the debate began.[165] The moderator asked Bob Malloy, the volume operator, to mute Reagan's microphone. After Breen repeated his demand to Malloy, Reagan furiously replied, "I am paying for this microphone, Mr. Green! [_[sic](/wiki/Sic "Sic")_]".[b][167] This turned out to be the turning point of the debate and the primary race.[168] Ultimately, the four additional candidates left, and the debate continued between Reagan and Bush. Reagan's polling numbers improved, and he won the New Hampshire primary by more than 39,000 votes.[169] Soon thereafter, Reagan's opponents began dropping out of the primaries, including Anderson, who left the party to become an independent candidate. Reagan easily captured the presidential nomination and chose Bush as his running mate at the [Detroit convention](/wiki/1980_Republican_National_Convention "1980 Republican National Convention") in July.[170]

The general election pitted Reagan against Carter amid the multitude of domestic concerns and ongoing [Iran hostage crisis](/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis "Iran hostage crisis") that began on November 4, 1979.[171] Reagan's campaign worried that Carter would be able to secure the release of the American hostages in [Iran](/wiki/Iran "Iran") as part of the [October surprise](/wiki/October_surprise "October surprise"),[172] Carter "suggested that Reagan would wreck [Social Security](/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\) "Social Security \(United States\)")" and portrayed him as a warmonger,[173] and Anderson carried support [from liberal Republicans](/wiki/Rockefeller_Republican "Rockefeller Republican") dissatisfied with Reagan's conservatism.[172][c] One of Reagan's key strengths was his appeal to the rising conservative movement. Though most conservative leaders espoused cutting taxes and budget deficits, many conservatives focused more closely on social issues like abortion and [gay rights](/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_the_United_States "LGBT rights in the United States").[175] Evangelical Protestants became an increasingly important voting bloc, and they generally supported Reagan.[176] Reagan also won the backing of [Reagan Democrats](/wiki/Reagan_Democrat "Reagan Democrat").[177] Though he advocated socially conservative viewpoints, Reagan focused much of his campaign on attacks against [Carter's foreign policy](/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Jimmy_Carter_administration "Foreign policy of the Jimmy Carter administration").[178]

In August, Reagan gave a [speech at the Neshoba County Fair](/wiki/States%27_rights_speech "States' rights speech"), stating his belief in [states' rights](/wiki/States%27_rights "States' rights"). Historians like [Joseph Crespino](/wiki/Joseph_Crespino "Joseph Crespino") argue that the visit was designed to incite racial animus in white Southern voters,[179] and some[_[who?](/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch")_] also saw these actions as an extension of the [Southern strategy](/wiki/Southern_strategy "Southern strategy") to garner white support for Republican candidates.[180] Allies and supporters of Reagan have said that this was his typical anti-big government rhetoric, without racial context or intent.[181][182] In the [October 28 debate](/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_debates "1980 United States presidential debates"), Carter chided Reagan for being against national health insurance. Reagan replied, "[There you go again](/wiki/There_you_go_again "There you go again")", though the audience laughed and viewers found him more appealing.[183] Reagan later asked the audience if they were better off than they were four years ago, slightly paraphrasing Roosevelt's words in 1934.[184] In 1983, Reagan's campaign managers were revealed to having [obtained Carter's debate briefing book](/wiki/Debategate "Debategate") before the debates.[185] On November 4, 1980, Reagan won in a decisive victory in the [Electoral College](/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College "United States Electoral College") over Carter, carrying 44 states and receiving 489 electoral votes to Carter's 49 in six states and the District of Columbia. He won the popular vote by a narrower margin, receiving nearly 51 percent to Carter's 41 percent and Anderson's 7 percent. Republicans [won a majority of seats in the Senate](/wiki/1980_United_States_Senate_elections "1980 United States Senate elections") for the first time since 1952[186] while Democrats [retained the House of Representatives](/wiki/1980_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections "1980 United States House of Representatives elections").[187]

## Presidency (1981–1989)

Main article: [Presidency of Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan "Presidency of Ronald Reagan")

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Ronald Reagan presidency](/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Ronald_Reagan_presidency "Timeline of the Ronald Reagan presidency").

Further information: [Domestic policy of the Ronald Reagan administration](/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_Ronald_Reagan_administration "Domestic policy of the Ronald Reagan administration") and [Foreign policy of the Ronald Reagan administration](/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Ronald_Reagan_administration "Foreign policy of the Ronald Reagan administration")

### First inauguration

Main article: [First inauguration of Ronald Reagan](/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Ronald_Reagan "First inauguration of Ronald Reagan")

[](/wiki/File:President_Ronald_Reagan_making_his_inaugural_address_from_the_United_States_Capitol.jpg)

Reagan delivers his inaugural address from the [US Capitol](/wiki/US_Capitol "US Capitol") (audio only).

Reagan was inaugurated as the 40th president of the United States on January 20, 1981.[188] [Chief Justice](/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_the_United_States "Chief Justice of the United States") [Warren E. Burger](/wiki/Warren_E._Burger "Warren E. Burger") administered the [presidential oath of office](/wiki/Oath_of_office_of_the_president_of_the_United_States "Oath of office of the president of the United States").[189] In [his inaugural address](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en:Ronald_Reagan%27s_First_Inaugural_Address "s:en:Ronald Reagan's First Inaugural Address"), Reagan commented on the country's economic malaise, arguing, "In this present crisis, government is not the solution to our problem, government is the problem".[190] As a final insult to President Carter, Iran waited until Reagan had been sworn in before announcing the release of their American hostages.[191][192]
